#237. Europe's car makers are in crisis. Will the threat of war rescue them?

Europe's car makers are in crisis. Will the threat of war rescue them?

It's green, bulging and intimidating. Ford's latest vehicle looks like a pick-up truck that has been through an Incredible Hulk transformation. Based on its popular Ranger series, this beefy number parked outside the front door of Ford's Dagenham plant can carry a load of two tonnes, tow up to four tonnes and is also shouldering the hopes of the 2,000-strong workforce that still makes engines here.

Inside the factory, the three-litre diesel engines that will power this camo-painted beast drift down a production line that has seen output cut from 90,000 engines a year to about half that over the last decade.

Ford is hoping that a pivot towards military vehicles can help fill the void created by what the company's UK boss calls the most challenging environment since the invention of the motor car. Once mighty, the European car industry is starting to look puny and is hoping surging defence budgets as Europe re-arms can help it flex its industrial muscles once more.

Ford is part of a bid for the British Army's Light Mobility Vehicle (LMV) programme

Ford is part of a joint venture with defence specialists General Dynamics and Ricardo bidding for a Ministry of Defence (MoD) contract to supply 9,000 vehicles over the next five to seven years to replace the Army's ageing Land Rover-based fleet.

Lisa Brankin, chair of Ford UK, says it is a chance to showcase their abilities to respond quickly to defence needs: "As a manufacturer you look at every opportunity that comes at you and this is a great opportunity that we would love to take advantage of."

This is not the first time Ford has busied itself with defence work.

Ahead of World War Two, Ford's factory in Dagenham, east London, was the largest car plant in Europe.

When war broke out, civilian car assembly stopped completely, and the factory was converted entirely to military production. Between 1939 and 1945, the Dagenham plant built 360,000 vehicles for the Allied war effort.

Ford workers in Manchester manufactured 34,000 Rolls-Royce-designed Merlin engines, which powered Spitfires and Hurricane fighter planes.

Eighty years later, the UK and European car industries are hoping that the engines of war can help defend against what one supplier told the BBC was a "terminal decline".

As Europe feels compelled to commit hundreds of billions to ramp up defence spending in the face of the menace from Russia and US reluctance to be Europe's protector, car makers that are under commercial attack from Chinese rivals have taken notice.

So can the rearmament of Europe save an auto industry and a supply chain facing crisis?

Ford is far from the only car company that sees defence as a growth industry - and one that can utilise the growing overcapacity at car plants across Europe.

French car maker Renault has signed a strategic agreement with defence giant Thales to produce military drones - targeting an output of up to 1,000 units per month. The French military and Directorate General for Armament want to tap into Renault's mass-production capabilities to bypass traditional, slower defence supply chains.

Meanwhile, Volkswagen has agreed to sell an under-used factory in Osnabruck, western Germany, which will become a military manufacturing hub in a joint venture with an Israeli-based defence investor.

Jaguar Land Rover (JLR), which makes the Land Rover, is also bidding for the same contract as Ford - as the Army retires its existing Land Rover-based fleet by 2030. JLR has also established a new dedicated business unit to support its global military ambitions.

It makes sense for under-utilised auto manufacturing capacity to switch to defence, says Mike Hawes of the Society for Motor Manufacturers and Traders (SMMT) trade association.

The UK car industry and its suppliers are heavily reliant on a few big manufacturers â Nissan in Sunderland, Toyota in Derbyshire, BMW in Oxfordshire and the biggest of them all by value of output, JLR at multiple sites in the Midlands and Merseyside.

And Hawes says the supply chain is very vulnerable.

"UK automotive output has been in decline over the last eight or nine years," he says. "We're probably half of what we were 10 years ago. Now, that's obviously going to hit the supply chain because they're not making the same number of parts. So they've got capacity. They may be quite dependent on one particular manufacturer as well."

So they will welcome "the opportunity to broaden their customer base and potentially move into defence", he says.

Just two weeks ago, JLR announced it was cutting 4,000 jobs from its 30,000-strong UK workforce in order to reduce costs to stay competitive with international â particularly Chinese â rivals. Dave Roberts of the firm Evtec, which supplies cooling systems components to JLR, is worried about the implications of this.

"JLR is the critical mass in the UK automotive manufacturing space," he says. "It is the glue that holds the whole of the sector together. Because remember, when you're making volumes for JLR, they're significantly higher than any other car maker in the UK.

"If they suffer, the ripples run deeper through the supply chain."

Earlier this week, major JLR suppliers urged the government to help automotive manufacturers move into aerospace and defence, warning that large-scale car production in the UK faces long-term decline.

Executives have warned of a "visible crack" in the UK automotive supply chain

In an open letter to the prime minister, chancellor and West Midlands Mayor Richard Parker, industry leaders argued Britain's automotive supply chain was "not in decline" but "in the wrong market".

The letter was signed by executives representing businesses with more than 8,600 direct employees, alongside the Confederation of British Metalforming, which represents about 75,000 workers.

Signatories to the letter said the redundancies were "the first visible crack" in a UK automotive supply chain supporting about 183,000 manufacturing jobs.

Across Europe, the industry is facing what Sigrid de Vries, director general of the ACEA auto industry manufacturers' association called "a perfect storm". Car makers are spending billions to shift to electric vehicles and insist that government sales targets for EVs are outpacing consumer demand.

And the EVs people are buying are increasingly from the biggest new threat to the industry â China.

In the early 2000s, Western firms saw China as a land of opportunity. The country's rapidly growing middle class had plenty of money and a seemingly insatiable appetite for cars, including the most profitable upmarket models. It was a lucrative business â the Chinese market once accounted for half of Volkswagen's profits.

However, it didn't last. China wanted a home-grown car industry and lavish state funding was ploughed into making the country a leader in high-tech businesses, including electric cars.

Today, the Chinese market is overloaded with brands, both foreign and domestic, and is characterised by cut-throat competition. Chinese car makers have focused their attention on foreign markets, and have used the transition to electric vehicles as a pathway to gaining market share. The likes of BYD, Chery and Geely are moving aggressively into Europe.

For European brands, all of this could not have happened at a worse time. The loss of the steady stream of profits from China, and the emergence of Chinese rivals on their home turf, has come after they invested heavily in producing electric vehicles (EVs). But EV sales have not increased as quickly as expected. Executives admit they have struggled to match the low production costs and development speed of the Chinese insurgents.

The result is that European manufacturers are now rushing to cut costs, while wondering what to do with expensive factories capable of producing millions more cars than they are able to sell.

Volkswagen has already announced plans to cut 100,000 jobs over the next few years. Whereas once closing plants in Germany would have been unthinkable, the company has now shuttered one in Dresden and may shut down four more. That includes a site in Zwickau, where VW spent over â¬1bn (Â£857m) converting production lines to build electric vehicles - a process that was completed just four years ago.

Industry estimates indicate that western European car plants have roughly 2.5 million vehicles worth of annual spare capacity.

Little wonder that car makers are jealously eyeing soaring defence budgets across Europe.

Sigrid de Vries says car makers are well placed to help Europe rearm.

"Many of the capabilities that defence needs are needed for and also delivered by the automotive sector," she says. "So automotive manufacturers and suppliers possess industrial assets, they possess manufacturing expertise, logistics capabilities, also advanced technologies. They have vast and also very integrated supply chains that may be relevant for Europe's broader defence preparedness objective."

But it is not that simple â security protocols, political and economic rivalries within Europe plus the fact that unless you're actually at war, the volumes will not replace the mass consumer market, all of which present challenges, she says.

"These are two very different worlds," she says. Governments want to invest in their defence capabilities and that is why it's now, more than ever, interesting for manufacturers and suppliers to see what's possible, but it will not be enough to address the underutilisation of manufacturing capacity we currently see."

If defence can't plug the yawning gaps in UK and European car manufacturing production â is it time for the henhouse to rent a room to the fox?

In other words, let Chinese companies move into European production facilities?

To offset the immense fixed costs of running idle assembly lines, legacy European and UK automakers are opening their doors to Chinese rivals.

Stellantis â owners of the Vauxhall, Fiat, Peugeot and Citroen marques â has taken a 20% stake in Chinese EV maker Leapmotor and production of the Chinese brand started in Poland two years ago. (Leapmotor then moved production to Spain after Poland voted to impose steep tariffs on Chinese EVs while Spain abstained â an example of how trade politics can get complicated.)

Nissan and Chery International UK have signed a non-binding Memorandum of Understanding for the study of contract manufacturing for Nissan to manufacture Chery vehicles at its Sunderland plant.

VW CEO Oliver Blume said in April of this year that Volkswagen was considering sharing spare European factory capacity with Chinese joint-venture partners.

In one sense it could be a win-win. European factories would have extra work while Chinese manufacturers could avoid high tariffs when selling into the US and the EU by making and exporting the cars in and from those markets.

But building cars in Europe does not automatically mean creating or saving European supply chains.

Some plants may only perform final assembly while many of the components â especially batteries â may continue to come from China.

Cultural treasures are being destroyed worldwide - so why is no-one being punished?

War may be coming. Are we psychologically ready?

Why a fresh wave of North Korean soldiers may fight in Russia's war in Ukraine

Car making is still seen as central to countries' sovereign manufacturing capability â in the old days the mantra was: "What's good for General Motors is good for America." You could say the same for Volkswagen, Mercedes and BMW for Germany and JLR for the UK.

Car plants are often the major employer in their area and support local supply chains, so job losses can hit particularly hard. That's why countries go to great lengths to protect and preserve them.

Australia is a case in point. When its final locally made car left the production line in 2017, it didn't just lose a car industry, it lost an engineering mindset and key skills, says Dave Roberts of Evtec.

"Over the next decade... ripple effects crept into all manufacturing," he says. "So they lost infrastructure capability. They lost advanced manufacturing capability.

"They're vulnerable. They're not resilient in those sectors anymore."

Ford UK's Lisa Brankin acknowledges 9,000 engines over five to seven years is a far cry from the 90,000 per year the plant used to produce â but she insists every little helps.

"It is a drop in the ocean but every single opportunity is worth having, isn't it?"

A spokesperson for the Ministry of Defence says it wants UK industry to play a "central role" in delivering thousands of modern light mobility vehicles. The spokesperson adds: "We are backing British businesses and supporting our defence industrial base with 85% of our defence spending currently staying in the UK, driving reindustrialisation and making defence an engine for growth."

It's understandable why a declining European car industry wants to gatecrash the spending splurge in the defence sector. But even if they can, right now it is hard to see how they'll ever be able to wield the muscle that they once did.

Correction 25 September: A graph that incorrectly implied there was a target for European defence spending to rise to â¬800bn by 2030 has been removed from this piece. It has been replaced by a line chart showing EU defence spending, both actual and projected, from 2018 to 2029.

#235. My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

The empty building that used to be the home of Marks & Spencer in Aberdeen is one of the first things you see when you arrive off the train and come into the city centre. The sign has gone, though the shape of the lettering can still be made out; the windows are blacked out and the doors boarded up.

This is my hometown. As a teenager, I spent hours on Union Street, its main thoroughfare, nicknamed the Granite Mile after the local silver-grey stone. It was the place to try on all the latest fashions from the likes of Clockhouse and Chelsea Girl.

The street used to be packed with shoppers from across the north-east of Scotland.

"I come to Union Street to take a bus somewhere else... there's nothing to shop for nowâ¦ it's horrendous," complains Marie Sim, a lifelong Aberdeen resident.

It's a similar story across the country: High Streets, once bursting with shops, hit first by the rise of out-of-town retail centres and then by online commerce.

But Aberdeen is in the thick of an ambitious turnaround plan. The port city has essentially declared war on its empty shop problem, taking an unusually hands-on approach to filling vacant units and bringing long-neglected upper floors of buildings into use.

The Marks & Spencer flagship store in Aberdeen closed in August, 80 years after it opened there

On top of that, the council is pouring tens of millions into trying to make the city centre a destination again. Like many other places, it's leaning into the "experience economy", with a new indoor food and drink market and a major redevelopment of the seafront.

High Streets are integral to how we feel about where we live. The sight of vacant shops and graffiti can quickly become a vicious circle, deterring shoppers, discouraging investment and leading to anti-social behaviour.

Politicians of all stripes have been trying for years to breathe new life into town centres - and Andy Burnham, who has made a focus on "place" part of his political identity, is the latest. He argues they should become "symbols of Britain's renaissance".

But can it be achieved? And if they're to survive, do town centres need a fundamental rethink?

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant, according to Green Street, the property and retail analytics company, a number that's stayed stubbornly high for years.

"If there was a silver bullet, it would have been fired years ago," says Jackie Sadek, a former government adviser on urban regeneration, who has spent nearly four decades working in town centre renewal.

One of the biggest obstacles is fractured ownership. Shops and buildings are often owned by dozens of different landlords, sometimes through offshore companies or complex structures, each with their own interests and priorities. That can slow decision-making and make large-scale regeneration difficult.

But even with empty units that are available, you need to find someone willing to take them on.

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant

The cost of running a High Street business has also risen; employment costs are higher, and there's the burden of business rates.

Firms need customers. Footfall on many High Streets has been in long-term decline as shopping and services move online, and this enormous structural shift has yet to play out fully.

Two-thirds of town centre experts think there is still 20% to 40% too much retail space, according to a survey by retail body Revo and real estate consultancy Lambert Smith Hampton.

In Aberdeen's boom years of North Sea oil, from the late 1970s through to the early 2010s, it didn't have to work hard to fill its hotels and restaurants and get the cash tills ringing because of the wealth the energy industry brought to the city.

I remember weekends with friends as a teenager, spending whatever cash we had in the fashion chains and shoe shops. Later came the pubs and bar-hopping, and trips to the art deco Capitol Theatre (now office space), where I saw Duran Duran.

But by the end of 2022, Union Street had hit rock bottom. Aberdonians had long deserted the High Street for a shopping centre, which opened in 2009 next to the railway station.

"They'd fallen out of love with their city," says Bob Keiller, head of Our Union Street coalition, a community-led group formed after an emergency summit to reverse the decline.

The first thing it did was listen to what residents wanted, receiving thousands of submissions. Restoring a bit of civic pride and filling empty units became the focus.

It's been a co-ordinated effort, involving letting agents, the council, landlords, businesses and a big team of volunteers, led by Keiller, a former FTSE 100 CEO, who isn't getting paid. He has a map on his office wall, detailing the status of every property, unit by unit, with colour-coded Post-it notes.

They audited the street, tracking down absent landlords, and logging what was empty and why. Volunteers offered to give the empty properties a clean and a lick of paint, to make them more attractive to let. They also spruced up the streets, picking litter, weeding, and painting bins. A small team still goes out once a fortnight.

Bob Keiller heads a community-led group set up to help revive Aberdeen's Union Street

There's a website where all the available properties are collated; crucially, all of the financial incentives are also listed. These can include council fit-out grants (where a new business gets money to decorate premises), and a two-year discount from business rates.

In 2023, 57 of the 198 units on Union Street were empty. As of today, 33 have been filled, with a further 14 in the process of being let.

But it can feel like a game of whack-a-mole because since the initiative started, another 20 units have become vacant. "The key thing is, we're filling quicker than emptying," Keiller insists.

Keiller's operations director, David McKeith, describes it as putting an arm round the shoulder of those they can help and being a "wasp in the ear" of people who just say no.

At one end of the office, a small, young team produces social media content, linking visitors and residents to a new Our Aberdeen app, showing what the city can offer.

For Keiller, the "small wins" - like tackling graffiti - are needed to help change public perception.

"We've realised the psychology is not to do it once but be prepared to do it two or three times before people that do these things eventually say, 'there's no point in putting it there because somebody's going to paint over it.'"

Attention is also turning to the often-forgotten upper floors of buildings along the once-iconic Union Street. In a separate initiative run by the business group Aberdeen Inspired, experts are getting inside those vacant high-up spaces to identify the barriers to bringing them back into use.

Aberdeen sought the help of a High Street troubleshooter, Iain Nicholson, who's worked with dozens of towns and cities through his Vacant Shops Academy.

Out of more than 1,000 empty units he helped audit nationally, on average more than half didn't have a letting agent actively marketing the property. He says some councils don't even know who the landlords are.

Empty shops, he says, won't all be filled by the market alone. Towns and cities need to encourage a broader range of occupiers, from arts and culture to leisure, health and well-being.

He says what's striking about Aberdeen is its joined-up approach, and the fact people "rolled up their sleeves" and overcame differences. "That's not something you can yet say about everywhere."

Every town and city faces its own mix of challenges.

Nicholson also helped Crewe in Cheshire. As a smaller place than Aberdeen, it is focusing on arts, culture, and community, with a new women's centre opened this month.

And English councils are being encouraged to take a more interventionist role.

They can now force an empty commercial unit that's been vacant for at least a year to be offered to tenants through a High Street Rental Auction, even if the landlord doesn't want to let it.

A noodle bar (left) is one of the new businesses in the area. Parts of the city centre have been undergoing construction work (right)

It's early days but the mere prospect of losing control of a property is delivering some results. Bassetlaw Council in Nottinghamshire, an early adopter of the scheme, says more than 30 shops across the district have been let or are currently under offer as a result of the policy.

But filling empty units is only part of the challenge. The bigger question is what town and city centres are actually for.

Changing habits mean we won't need so many shops. Instead, it's argued, town centres will need more people living and working in them, with new homes and office space alongside services that can't be done online. With an ageing population, for example, bringing NHS health centres into the heart of a town could be a big opportunity.

In Aberdeen, the building that used to house BHS - a department store shuttered in 2016 - is being converted into an entrance plaza for the new food and drink market.

The physical appearance of Union Street is changing too. Like many Victorian shopping thoroughfares, it was built with a wide road (probably to give space to horses and carriages). Now parts of the pavements are being widened to make it a more pleasant place to spend time, though many Aberdonians are weary of the disruption, and of new traffic restrictions.

Money is a big barrier. Aberdeen's new indoor food and drink market is costing Â£40m, funded by the council and the UK Levelling Up Fund. In other places, regeneration schemes can struggle because the cost of redevelopment outweighs the likely financial return, making support from the public sector essential. And the economics are becoming more challenging.

"Higher interest rates, rising costs of construction and other issues are impacting the viability and delivery of new housing and big redevelopment projects," says Steve Norris, a director at Lambert Smith Hampton. He says that big projects usually require cash from both the public and private sectors.

He wants to see "more creative ways of funding".

Part of Burnham's plan is to give local leaders more freedom to invest in town centres and drive regeneration, building on the approach he has championed in Greater Manchester. But bigger projects take years.

Volunteers have played a role in the redevelopment of Aberdeen's Union Street

Making it easier and cheaper to open and run a High Street business is one of the more immediate solutions being called for, with business rates a particular concern.

Burnham has promised further rates reform. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go in England. Business rates are devolved in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.

The chief executive of Next, Simon Wolfson, argues that the bigger challenge is not business rates, but planning reform.

"The most important thing that the government can do for high streets is let them develop," says Wolfson, who's also a Conservative peer.

Back in Aberdeen, there are some signs of traction.

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street in 2024. A council grant helped with the refurbishment after they decided to make the leap.

Can they make the sums add up? "We're getting there," she says.

She feels the same about the future of her street. "I always say Rome wasn't built in a day."

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street with the help of a council grant

Right now the area feels like a construction site - but another gaping hole will soon be filled. Robert Keane is a local property developer who in 2023 bought the Trinity shopping centre, once home to Debenhams. The space is now set to be re-occupied by a national leisure brand. A notoriously tiring stairway leading to the Trinity from the train station is also being replaced by an escalator.

"We saw a real good opportunity, because it was dying," he says.

He reckons he can make a go of it, cutting the cost of parking and overheads, resetting rents and being more nimble than previous far-flung owners.

It was a bold move given Lambert Smith Hampton suggests that more than one in 10 UK shopping centres is ripe for demolition.

But he's betting the dynamics of Aberdeen are finally changing.

"I think by 2027 it should look and feel like a different city."

That is certainly the hope. Yet walking along Union Street today, past the boarded-up shopfronts and roadworks, it can still feel like a city caught between its past and whatever comes next.

How the High Street became a window on our political instability

Why the UK is dithering over what to do about e-scooters

The sale of illegal cigarettes signals a deeper problem with UK high streets

#234. Trump's 2028 successors are already fighting over who to blame for GOP midterm wipeout

Republicans are still reckoning with a historic midterm wipeout, but the fight to assign blame and inherit President Donald Trump's political movement is already underway.

Vice President JD Vance faults the war in Iran. Secretary of State Marco Rubio defends it. Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis says Republicans fought the wrong battles. Texas Sen. Ted Cruz, R-Texas, argues the party lost touch with voters' economic struggles.

The competing visions from potential presidential contenders offer an early glimpse of the Republican infighting ahead of 2028. But all face the same dilemma: How to convince voters the party can change course without alienating Trump or the MAGA base they need to win the nomination.

The biggest obstacle may be Trump himself, who has shown little appetite for a political retreat.

On Monday, Trump made a surprise appearance in the White House briefing room, declaring the Iran war "militarily is effectively over" while maintaining that the U.S. remains prepared to resume strikes. Earlier that day, he questioned the legitimacy of Republicans' midterm defeat, suggesting without evidence that voting machines were responsible, days after saying that after years of election denial, he would accept the 2026 results.

Since the election, Trump has also replaced Chief of Staff Susie Wiles with immigration hard-liner Stephen Miller and backed Republican National Committee Chairman Joe Gruters against a challenger demanding an accounting of the party's losses.

Trump is also keeping his would-be successors guessing. In a CBS interview Sunday, he praised Rubio alongside Vance but suggested he might stay out of the 2028 Republican primary.

"Maybe I'm better off not getting involved," he said, while emphasizing the weight his endorsement could carry.

That dynamic leaves Vance in a precarious position. Entering the midterms as the clear political heir, Vance now faces a critical question: Can he inherit MAGA without inheriting the blame for Trump's presidency?

In a lengthy post on X Wednesday as election results were still trickling in, Vance challenged Trump's initial explanation for the Republican defeat.

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance wrote, arguing voters who backed the Trump-Vance ticket in 2024 abandoned Republicans in 2026 over Iran.

Pointing to foreign policy allows Vance to criticize a defining administration decision without repudiating the broader MAGA movement, while echoing his longstanding opposition to foreign interventions. Vance also has national name recognition and donor ties to help his prospective candidacy.

But the election results complicate Vance's argument.

Just 34% of House voters called the war crucial to their vote, versus 49% citing the cost of living and 55% government corruption, according to the CNBC Voter Poll – suggesting a broader backlash against the Trump administration that Vance has spent nearly two years defending.

"You can't campaign against a war from within an administration you serve," conservative commentator Joel Pollak wrote on X, questioning how Vance could distance himself from a conflict he had publicly defended.

In his CBS interview, Trump said he did not disagree with Vance's assessment and acknowledged the vice president had discussed the post with him beforehand. But he insisted the decision to attack Iran was right and suggested Vance also has losses of his own to explain.

After Vance spent months fundraising and campaigning as Republican National Committee finance chairman, Democrats won both marquee races in his home state of Ohio. Amy Acton defeated Vance ally Vivek Ramaswamy for governor, after the vice president defended a controversial ad featuring Acton's estranged mother. Former Sen. Sherrod Brown claimed Vance's former Senate seat, defeating incumbent Republican Jon Husted.

Rep. Randy Fine, R-Fla., stated Sunday he would not support Vance in 2028 due to his war criticism, calling it a betrayal of Trump. "You have to be loyal to those people who brought you to the dance," Fine said in an interview with LiveNOW from FOX on Sunday, naming Rubio and Cruz as alternatives.

Rubio is making a different bet: Defend the Iran war while distancing himself from the Republican defeat.

"Sometimes, when you do the right thing, you don't know whether it's popular or not, but it's still the right thing to do," Rubio told reporters Sunday before departing for France.

Rubio, who has denied he plans to run in 2028, largely avoided the campaign trail and has stayed out of the immediate blame game. But his dual role as secretary of state and national security advisor ties him directly to Trump's foreign policy record, which could face renewed scrutiny when Democrats take control of the House. The House may vote this week on articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth in a rebuke of the Trump administration's foreign policy.

DeSantis, who is not in the Trump administration, is questioning the party's entire playbook.

The outgoing Florida governor called the midterms "close to an extinction-level event" for Republicans at a news conference Wednesday in Tallahassee, Florida, accusing Washington of failing to deliver on its promises while fighting culture wars that no longer resonated with voters.

"Republicans ... really were fighting yesterday's battle," DeSantis said at the news conference.

It was a striking critique from a governor who built his national profile on those same fights.

DeSantis can point to Republicans' ability to retain control of Florida even as Democrats flipped longtime GOP strongholds elsewhere. But Republican Byron Donalds won the governor's race by just 1.3 points, while Democrats gained ground in the Legislature.

Still, Rep. Greg Steube, R-Fla., credited DeSantis with building a "massive sea wall against the blue tsunami" in an X post.

The scramble to succeed Trump extends well beyond Vance, Rubio and DeSantis.

Cruz called the midterms a "Watergate-level repudiation" in a post on X Wednesday, blaming Trump's tariffs and the prolonged Iran war. Other potential 2028 contenders include Georgia Gov. Brian Kemp, Arkansas Gov. Sarah Huckabee Sanders and former Virginia Gov. Glenn Youngkin, who face the same challenge of moving beyond Trump without alienating his base.

Some wealthy Republican donors are already looking beyond Vance, weighing DeSantis and other alternatives amid concerns that a candidate from Trump's administration would inherit the political baggage of his second term and low voter approval ratings.

The divisions extend to Trump's own coalition. Among voters who backed Republican House candidates, roughly two-thirds of those under 30 approved of Trump's performance, much less than the nearly nine in 10 voters 65 and older, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Younger Republicans were also more skeptical of the Iran war and U.S. support for Israel, according to the exit poll data.

"If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so," Vance said Wednesday. "Agree or disagree, that's at least honest. But everyone knows why we lost."

#232. Poppi founders are going after PepsiCo's Gatorade with new beverage startup Jetty

After disrupting the soda category with Poppi, founders Allison and Stephen Ellsworth are now taking aim at hydration with their new brand, Jetty.

Starting Monday, Jetty drinks and ready-to-mix electrolyte packets will be on shelves at Whole Foods grocery stores and available online at Target and Amazon.

The new, self-funded venture directly pits the Ellsworths against PepsiCo, which bought Poppi for $1.95 billion in 2025. Pepsi owns Gatorade, which dominates the U.S. sports drink category with about 60% market share, according to Numerator data.

Poppi shook up the soda category with prebiotic fiber, low calorie and sugar counts and its fun marketing aimed at Generation Z and millennials. Jetty looks to upend the hydration category through a similar strategy. To do so, it will face off not only against Gatorade but also a swarm of other upstarts looking to get in on the electrolyte-fueled trend.

For decades, Gatorade and other sports drinks have pitched their products to athletes who need electrolytes and fluids after an intense workout, although many consumers drink them every day without even sweating. Jetty is aimed at people who need a hydration boost, but not the same level of electrolytes or sugar as a traditional sports drink.

"No one has really created a product for just everyday hydration — literally everyday hydration for normal people," Allison Ellsworth, Jetty's chief brand officer, told CNBC. "Like I woke up this morning, I didn't run a marathon."

Following Poppi's sale to PepsiCo, Ellsworth stayed on as an advisor to the brand, although she said she is no longer involved in the day-to-day operations.

After 10 years building Poppi, Ellsworth said, she finally had free time. As Ellsworth tells it, she went to the doctor for the first time in a decade. She started using a GLP-1 medication and lost 60 pounds.

Ellsworth's husband, Stephen, now CEO of the new brand, noticed she wasn't hydrating, even when she came back from the gym.

GLP-1 medications such as Ozempic and Mounjaro can make people less thirsty. But because staying hydrated can also help mitigate some of the drugs' side effects, such as nausea and headaches, it can become more important.

"I physically struggle getting liquid into my body," Ellsworth said.

Plain old water is often less appealing to consumers, even dehydrated GLP-1 users, but more flavorful options with added benefits can be tempting. About 44% of GLP-1 users report consuming more functional beverages, according to EY-Parthenon's third annual survey of more than 3,600 current or former GLP-1 users.

Ellsworth sees a broader appeal for Jetty, too.

"Our core audience is going to be millennial moms and Gen Z," she said. "Gen Z just isn't relating to these old school brands, the legacy of the Gatorades and the Powerades. They're just not cool."

Consumers broadly have become more interested in staying hydrated.

Dollar sales of hydration and electrolyte products climbed 20% to $2.6 billion over the 52 weeks ended Oct. 4, based on data from SPINS, which tracks new universal product codes, or UPCs. The category also has a strong repeat buyer rate of 62%.

However, the Ellsworths aren't the only ones to notice the potential in hydration. Big food and beverage companies have been looking to hydration, too.

Kraft Heinz debuted Capri Sun juice pouches and Kool-Aid packets with electrolytes this spring. In April, Pepsi unveiled an overhaul of the Gatorade brand, focused on making it more appealing to a wider swath of consumers, with more potential occasions like a "fresh morning start" or an "afternoon walk." And Electrolit is a top performer for its distributor Keurig Dr Pepper, with 40% retail sales growth in the company's third quarter.

All of that means more competition for shelf space and consumers' "share of throat," an industry term for beverage market share.

To increase Jetty's visibility across the grocery store and to maximize convenience for shoppers, Jetty will be available in both powders and bottles.

Single-use electrolyte mixes are growing at a much faster clip than the ready-to-drink category, Ellsworth said, thanks to Unilever's Liquid I.V. and smaller upstarts like LMNT. On Amazon alone, powders are a $1 billion category, according to Ellsworth.

The ready-to-drink, or RTD, sports beverages are a much larger market, but the category's sales are shrinking as consumers reach for other hydration options instead. Unit sales of sports drinks have fallen 9.1% from 2022 to 2026, according to Numerator data. And Coca-Cola has written down the value of BodyArmor by more than $1.7 billion after buying control of the brand for $5.6 billion in late 2021.

"Me and my husband know how to do RTD," Ellsworth said.

Jetty will launch with five flavors, including watermelon, orange mango, fruit punch, black raspberry and white cherry. All of them contain 100 milligrams of sodium, 310 milligrams of potassium and 100 milligrams of magnesium in a 15.2-ounce bottle or 15-gram packet.

In addition to electrolytes, Jetty products also contain B vitamins, 3 grams of fiber and 6 grams of sugar. It doesn't use artificial sweeteners or dyes, its founders say.

For comparison, a 20-ounce bottle of traditional Gatorade Thirst Quencher has about 270 milligrams of sodium and 80 milligrams of potassium, plus 34 grams of sugar. The sports drink brand is also phasing out artificial colors and promoting lower-sugar versions.

"What's really interesting about this product and this opportunity is we now can capture all those lapsed drinkers — or that generation that didn't even ever drink it in the first place," Ellsworth said.

Poppi's new owner knows about Jetty and the market it is trying to capture, too.

"I don't think [Pepsi] gets it, but what they do get is that the movement is changing, and they think that just by taking dyes out or doing 90% less sugar, they're going to capture this audience," Ellsworth said. "But that is very big corporate thinking."

She added that she believes Pepsi has missed the mark on advertising to Gen Z consumers across its portfolio, such as in its campaigns for Sprite competitor Starry.

Perhaps as an extension of that, Poppi's performance has been underwhelming under PepsiCo's ownership.

The food and beverage giant now estimates the fair value of potential performance-based payments to Poppi's prior owners is $12 million, Pepsi said in a regulatory filing in October. At the time of the deal, Pepsi promised an additional payment of $300 million if Poppi hit certain targets by the third quarter of 2027. The Ellsworths declined to comment on the filing to CNBC.

This time around, with one successful brand under their belts, Jetty's founders say they have learned from past mistakes. Those lessons should help Jetty as it launches at a much larger scale than Poppi did.

Three different claims lawyers have signed off on Jetty's packaging, according to Ellsworth. Poppi paid $8.9 million to settle class-action litigation alleging false advertising over its "gut healthy" claims.

For now, Jetty is entirely self-funded with $2.5 million, although Ellsworth said that the brand will likely raise money in 2027. This time, though, she said investors will probably look less like traditional venture capitalists.

"I want to bring in investors that bring value," Ellsworth said. Instead of a venture capitalist who is willing to invest $15 million, Jetty would rather consider investors who can only write a check for $1 million but have access to celebrities. "So then you're getting access to talent on a cheaper level and people that are invested in the brand."

And Jetty has also learned a few lessons from Gatorade. For example, the brand is already at work on a lemon-lime version — the original flavor of Gatorade.

"We'll eventually do it," Ellsworth said. "But it was really important at the beginning just to stick to flavors that we love, for it to taste good."

#231. New regulation for heating oil market, but no price controls

Home heating oil suppliers in Northern Ireland will face stricter regulation in the wake of an inquiry by the UK's competition watchdog.

However it will not include the sort of price controls applied to the gas and electricity markets.

The Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) investigated the heating oil market in Northern Ireland and other parts of the UK., external

The Economy Minister Caoimhe Archibald said she will now act on the CMA's recommendations.

"The CMA identified the need for stronger consumer protection, support for vulnerable households, and more formal dispute resolution arrangements," she told MLAs.

She said the changes she was announcing are "proportionate" and will enhance existing heating oil consumer protection "at minimal additional cost".

The measures include a mandatory code of conduct for heating oil suppliers and giving the Consumer Council an enhanced role in helping consumers resolve disputes.

The Consumer Council will also lead in the creation of an opt-in register for vulnerable consumers.

About two thirds of Northern Ireland households use home heating oil, by far the highest of any UK region.

At the start of the US-Israel attack on Iran in March, heating oil prices in Northern Ireland rose by a record monthly rate of 92%.

That compared to the previous largest spike of 59% in March 2022, after the Russian invasion of Ukraine.

Analysis by the CMA suggested that prices rose even more steeply in Scotland and Wales, where heating oil is typically used in remote rural areas.

It found that prices tended to be lower in Northern Ireland overall as it has a competitive range of suppliers and a market which is big enough to minimise delivery costs.

The CMA said that the huge spike in prices at the start of the Iran conflict largely reflected rising wholesale costs.

It did not recommend price controls suggesting they could be counterproductive.

The government said Â£5m will help "off grid" homes using oil or LPG and are eligible for reduced council tax

Up to 20,000 homes using heating oil could get a Â£250 payment from the Welsh government to help with the rising cost of living.

The government said Â£5m from a new Â£130m package will help "off grid" households who are dependent on either heating oil or LPG and are eligible for reduced council tax.

The package includes the Â£100 household energy payment, with this extended to those eligible for council tax relief "irrespective of band".

It also includes Â£7m support for bus travel and Â£8m for councils to help those "not captured by other schemes".

It follows the UK government unveiling a Â£100 "household energy payment" for council taxpayers in bands A-D in England in October.

Wales' Deputy First Minister Sioned Williams said supporting families with the cost of living was a "key priority".

"We understand that this is a challenging time for many households across Wales," she said.

"In total this represents an additional investment of Â£130m, providing immediate relief for households this winter. While we are taking firm action wherever we can, we need the UK Government to play its part".

Five million pounds of the package will help towards "off-grid" fuel costs with a Â£250 payment available to households eligible for Council Tax Reduction based on their incomes and are dependent on either heating oil or LPG.

An additional Â£8m will allow councils to extend the Â£100 payment to households "not captured" by other schemes and are facing financial hardship.

While Â£7m is being made available to support bus operators with their high fuel costs and protect passengers from fares increases.

The Welsh government said with winter approaching and the ongoing conflict in the Middle East, "the coming months will be a challenging time for many".

Deputy First Minister Sioned Williams says families are facing a "challenging time"

#225. Some pupils 'struggling just to get a hot meal'

Robert-Frederic said school dinner price hikes had made paying for food "more difficult for all students"

The increasing price of school dinners in Northern Ireland has left some pupils "struggling just to get a hot meal," one teenage student has said.

Robert-Frederic, 17, is among pupils at Belfast's Malone Integrated College who spoke out after it was announced prices are to rise for the second year in a row.

Post-primary pupils will pay an extra 15% on their canteen choices from January, while set meals at pre-school, primary and special schools will rise by 30p.

The Education Authority (EA) said the amount parents would have to pay was still well below the average cost of producing a school meal, estimated at over Â£5.20.

Families recently had to set aside extra money for term-time meals after primary and special school dinners increased by 50p in January 2026.

At that time, post-primary pupils were informed they would pay almost 20% more for the food they bought in school canteens.

Robert-Frederic told BBC News NI that the previous increase had already made paying for lunches "more difficult for all students".

"Instead of just going off numbers on a page, you should actually see how it affects people."

Malone Integrated College pupil Naomi said lack of proper food can affect learning

More than half (55%) of pupils at Malone Integrated College are entitled to free school meals but many others are from low incomes households.

"It's horrible seeing people around me not being able to afford school meals," said 17-year-old Naomi.

"It also puts pressure on the parents," she added.

"Without proper lunches it can affect people's learning and their mental health."

For 16-year-old Paddy, the last price hike meant he had to change his canteen choices at Malone.

"Some of my favourite foods such as like the school chicken - that went up," he said.

"It was around Â£2.20 and now it's Â£2.60.

"And as a student that felt like I should prioritise getting to school because I only get Â£5 a day and Â£2.50 of that has to go towards getting the bus."

Paddy said he was having to choose between his favourite foods and his bus ticket

The school's headteacher, Ãine Leslie, said the price rise will put further pressure on families who are already worried about the cost of living crisis.

"I think the cumulative effect of this will be immense, particularly families who have multiple children, both at a primary school and a post-primary school, and trying to make ends meet, it's going to become incredibly difficult," she said.

When the prices increased earlier this year, she remembers "sitting with the head of the cafeteria and heartbreakingly decide how much to put up each item".

She said some students would be "worried about the stigma attached to this, and won't voice their concerns and won't ask for help" which was "really sad".

Leslie added that not having a healthy, balanced diet or going without food will be a "barrier to learning".

Principal Ãine Leslie said she believes "families are going to struggle"

The latest increase means the price of a paid for dinner in a primary school or special school, for instance, will have risen from Â£2.60 to Â£3.40 since the start of 2026.

Pupils eligible for free school meals will not be affected.

The rise in school dinner prices is not the only measure introduced by the EA in response to what they describe as "an exceptionally challenging financial position".

There will also be a change to some menus in schools, replacing baked desserts with cheaper alternatives.

Prices went up at the start of 2026 and will increase again in the new year

Education Minister Paul Givan said he was disappointed and concerned but added it was due to underfunding of the education sector as a whole.

"I don't want this to be introduced, but it is a result of the Education Authority not having sufficient funding," he told the BBC's Good Morning Ulster programme.

He said education often bears "the greatest burden" during cost-saving exercises and argued his department should receive a greater share of Stormont's budget.

Givan also said the "inadequate" funding that Stormont receives from Westminster was "bearing down on a huge number of public services in Northern Ireland".

Stormont ministers are yet to agree a budget, despite weeks of talks with Secretary of State Sir Chris Bryant, who has repeatedly said there is "no more money".

Christos Gaitatzis is the principal of Omagh High

At Omagh High School in County Tyrone, almost 30% of pupils have free school meals.

For the families who have to pay, putting the price up could deter those children having "nutritious meals" according to its principal, Christos Gaitatzis.

He told BBC News NI that the 15% hike was excessive.

"What is the price index at the moment â is it 3,4, 5%? I would imagine that would be the reasonable increase that you would put on anything.

"So they are going three times the value - to me it is unreasonable."

He said the rise would have an "overall future impact on the health of the kids".

The commissioner for children, Chris Quinn, says children should not have to pay the price for the financial crisis in education

Northern Ireland's Commissioner for Children and Young People, Chris Quinn, said he was ''flabbergasted" at the change.

"School meals are not a luxury. For many children they are an essential part of the school day, and these increases will hit hardest those families already struggling with the cost of living."

Quinn added it was "particularly difficult to accept" another price hike shortly after the EA advised staff and pupils to "wear more clothes" to cut school heating bills.

"Children did not create the financial crisis in education and they should not be expected to pay the price for it," he added.

Payments which subsidise milk at about 500 schools are at risk, but the EA is to seek funding from the Department of Agriculture, Environment and Rural Affairs.

The EA is also planning to offer more parents payments to transport their children to school rather than paying for taxis.

At present, some parents receive payments if their children are eligible for free school transport but no private providers, like taxis, are available.

Charges for EA music lessons for pupils are also set to rise again.

The EA said it was being asked to deliver services "with insufficient funding" at a time when costs, such as providing energy for schools, continued to rise.

It said it hoped to save Â£20m by introducing the measures on top of Â£30m saved this year.

"These were difficult decisions which sought to balance our commitment to children and young people alongside the requirement to live within the funding allocated for the year."

#222. Law on 'holiday hunger' passed by Stormont assembly

Community and voluntary groups have been publicly supportive of the Holiday Meal Payments Bill, with some staging a demonstration at Stormont on Monday

A law aimed at helping lower income families with food costs during school holidays has been passed by the Northern Ireland Assembly.

The Holiday Meal Payments Bill seeks to create a new payment for families whose children receive free school meals to help with food costs outside of term time.

A majority of 49 to 24 assembly members backed the legislation, which was proposed by Sinn FÃ©in's Danny Baker and welcomed by some parents and charities.

Education Minister Paul Givan described the bill as "fiscally reckless", warning it would cost Â£20m to Â£30m a year and lead to cuts elsewhere within his Stormont department.

The Holiday Meal Payments Bill was brought forward by Sinn FÃ©in assembly member Danny Baker

Baker said that "poverty isn't green and orange" and the passage of the bill would support thousands of children "across all of our communities".

"When children are returning to school hungry, then they're not learning," he told assembly members during the bill's final stage debate.

"If you want to deal with educational underachievement then you have to deal with poverty."

Givan described the bill as "well intentioned" but said "sincerity is not a funding model".

The Democratic Unionist Party (DUP) minister added there was "no such thing as a free lunch" and the legislation "will require cuts to be made" to other services.

"So I ask those who support this bill, what cuts should be made to afford this payment?"

The minister argued efforts at Stormont to tackle poverty should be made "in a coordinated way" across the entire four-party executive.

Last month, Givan's department tabled amendments that would have given him power to delay its implementation, but these were rejected by a majority of parties.

Paul Givan challenged supporters of the bill to identify which educational services should be cut in order to fund the holiday hunger payments

Stormont assembly members voted on the bill on Tuesday night after more than four hours of debate during its final stage in the chamber.

Alliance Party assembly member Nick Mathison, chair of the assembly's education committee, said "holiday hunger" remained a significant issue for many families.

He acknowledged Stormont's financial pressures but said the funding required for the bill was a "relatively small" proportion of the Department of Education's budget.

"When we are in a budgetary crisis we need to be ensuring that we do not end up penalising those who are least able to bear the costs," he added.

Ulster Unionist Party (UUP) leader Jon Burrows commended Baker on the sentiment of his private member's bill but warned "there is no funding stream and there is no money".

He said the bill "lacks any governance", adding: "There are no guarantees the money given under this will end up in nutritious meals inside the stomachs of children."

"It's a fundamental misapplication and misunderstanding of how the welfare system should work," he told assembly members.

Social Democratic and Labour Party (SDLP) assembly member Cara Hunter welcomed the bill, saying that "a child can't learn on an empty stomach".

"Hunger shows up in concentration, in behaviour and attainment long before it shows up in the spreadsheets or the speeches that we have before us here today," she added.

Baker said he was motivated to introduce it by some of his experiences of breakfast clubs and meal schemes in youth work.

"There was a young boy, about the same age of my youngest, around seven years old," he explained.

"He was filling his wee pockets up and we caught on to this.

"To cut a long story short, he was bringing home food for his siblings because there just wasn't enough food in the house."

Baker said all ministers had choices to make and the legislation alone could not solve child poverty, but would act as an "early intervention".

Children are eligible for free school meals if their family's household income is below Â£15,390 a year.

Northern Ireland is the only UK region without government-funded holiday food support, after the Department of Education (DE) ended its Â£27-per-fortnight holiday meal grant in 2023.

Baker's legislation was introduced in the Northern Ireland Assembly in March and has been scrutinised by members of the legislative assembly (MLAs) on a number of occasions over the past nine months.

Louise McCullough is a mum-of-three who cannot afford to work full time because of the cost of childcare

Community and voluntary groups have been publicly supportive of the bill, with some staging a demonstration at Stormont on Monday in a bid to urge MLAs to get it over the finish line.

Louise McCullough, a mum-of-three from east Belfast, was among those who spoke at the event.

She said her children receive free school meals and that she would be directly affected by the legislation.

"There are so many working families who need this, it will change their lives and be massively impactful," she told BBC News NI.

"I can't afford to work full time because of the cost of childcare, and the cost of living.

"We have to do what we can and schemes like free school meals make a difference but so much more needs done... if you can't find money for hungry children what are you doing?

"Our government should be scaffolding our families properly."

Edel Diamond is from the South West Belfast Foodbank said the new law would make a big difference to the families she comes across

Edel Diamond from the South West Belfast Foodbank said organisations like hers had been "filling in the gaps" to help struggling families.

"We shouldn't have to - it's incumbent on our executive to put these initiatives forward," she said.

"This will make such a difference to families we're seeing daily."

#221. FDA warns of vision loss risk linked to popular GLP-1 drugs like Wegovy, Zepbound

The U.S. Food and Drug Administration is requiring new safety warnings on all GLP-1 drugs after finding evidence of a potential increased risk of a rare eye condition, the agency said on Monday.

The condition, which can cause sudden vision loss, known as nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION), is rare, and a causal relationship has not been established.

The FDA is requiring the makers of all GLP-1 medications to add a warning about the potential increased risk of NAION associated with use of these medications, it said.

NAION is a condition that can result in sudden decreased vision, including permanent vision loss. It is caused by decreased blood flow to the front part of the ​optic nerve — the underlying cause is not completely understood, however.

NAION cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 products, the FDA said.

Ozempic and Wegovy maker Novo Nordisk said it "continues to believe the data do not establish a cause-and-effect relationship, and we remain confident in the benefit-risk profile of our GLP-1 medicines."

CNBC has reached out to Eli Lilly, the company behind Mounjaro, Zepbound and Foundayo, for comment.

Diabetes itself is a known risk factor for NAION, complicating efforts to determine if cases among GLP-1 users are caused by the medications or by patients' underlying health conditions.

The FDA decision follows a review of clinical trials, real-world patient data, and published studies.

An analysis of 126 trials involving more than 134,000 patients found 6.4 cases of NAION per 100,000 patient-years among GLP-1 users, compared with 3.8 among patients receiving other treatments or placebos.

The FDA said it identified 15 probable NAION events, 10 in patients treated with GLP-1s and five in patients receiving a placebo, and no cases were identified among patients receiving other non-GLP-1 glucose-lowering medications.

Several observational studies also identified an increased risk, supporting the FDA's concerns.

The agency's findings were more mixed when comparing the risk of developing NAION from the use of GLP-1 drugs versus other commonly prescribed diabetes drugs.

Real-world patient data found a statistically significant increase in the risk of NAION among GLP-1 users compared with patients taking DPP-4 inhibitors, another class of diabetes medications, but not compared with those taking SGLT-2 inhibitors, per the FDA. SGLT-2 inhibitors are another class of medicines that includes Jardiance and Farxiga.

However, observational studies consistently reported an increased risk of NAION in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus who started a GLP-1 treatment compared to those who initiated DPP-4 inhibitors or SGLT-2 inhibitors, the FDA added.

The FDA's class-wide labeling action is "likely to attract attention but should have limited impact on GLP-1 uptake," according to Citi analysts.

It may temporarily increase patient and investor concern, but the rarity of NAION and substantial weight, glycemic, cardiovascular, renal, and sleep apnea benefits should preserve a favorable benefit-risk profile and limit changes to prescribing the medicines, they added.

While GLP-1 drugs have been shown to lead to benefits beyond weight loss and controlling blood sugar levels, many also carry the FDA's strongest safety warning, known as a boxed warning, over a potential risk of thyroid tumors.

Studies have shown that semaglutide, the active ingredient in Wegovy and Ozempic, and tirzepatide, the active ingredient in Mounjaro, cause thyroid C-cell tumors in rodents. It remains unknown whether the drugs cause these tumors, including a rare form of thyroid cancer, in humans.

Both semaglutide and tirzepatide also carry warnings about diabetic retinopathy, another eye condition that can lead to blurred vision, difficulty seeing in low light, and, eventually, sight loss. Diabetic retinopathy is when high blood sugar damages the tiny blood vessels in the retina, the light-sensitive tissue at the back of the eye.

In a two-year trial involving patients with type 2 diabetes and high cardiovascular risk, diabetic retinopathy complications occurred in 3% of patients treated with Ozempic, compared with 1.8% of those receiving a placebo, according to the drug's prescribing information.

Mounjaro's FDA label separately warns that rapid improvements in blood sugar control have been associated with temporary worsening of diabetic retinopathy and recommends monitoring patients with a history of the condition.

European regulators flagged a risk of NAION associated with semaglutide in June 2025.

The European Medicines Agency's safety committee concluded that NAION was a very rare side effect of semaglutide that may affect up to 1 in 10,000 people taking it, and recommended updating the drug's product information. The U.K.'s medicines regulator followed with a similar warning in February.

Unlike the FDA's latest action, which covers all GLP-1 medications, the European and British warnings focused specifically on semaglutide.

#220. The AI telling farmers when to harvest

Choosing the right moment to harvest can be a make-or-break decision

When the first day of the apple harvest rolled around in Washington State last year, the fruit was ripe and the pickers were ready â but the weather had other ideas.

"It was like 38Câ¦ it's not safe for people to work in that heat," recalls Joel Carter at Okanagan Specialty Fruits. "We had to stop at 10 o'clock in the morning."

He says that artificial intelligence (AI) models that forecast ideal harvest dates, taking into account the weather, would be useful. "You need to know more than just when your fruit is going to be ripe. How long do you have to pick it?" he says. "That's where these models are really helpful."

Carter's company has more than 1,250 acres of apple orchards in Washington, and the fruit is grown for sliced apple portions â often sold to hotels and schools, for example. The firm is investing in technology with the hope of maximising productivity. Even the apples are genetically engineered so that they don't brown easily once cut.

But planning a harvest is tricky. New tools that count and analyse fruit on the tree or vine, and predict when crops will ripen, are emerging. It matters because prices for fruit, especially high-value berries such as strawberries or blueberries can fluctuate wildly. Getting the harvest date wrong means you book seasonal workers when you don't actually need them, and risk missing out on the biggest profits.

Okanagan Specialty Fruits is already experimenting with cameras from a Canadian firm called Vivid Machines. The cameras are mounted atop tractors and they scoop up imagery of the apple trees as the tractor trundles by. AI identifies buds, flowers or fruit in that footage.

"Right now, Vivid is telling us crop estimates and harvest dates," says Carter. He notes that the system is good at picking out very tiny flower buds, which are hard to see at a glance with the naked eye.

But the accuracy of forecasts is noticeably dependent on the quality of historical information fed in to the system, adds Carter. "This isn't something where an AI can scrape the internet and figure out what's the average [yield] for Granny Smith," he explains. "It's going to be bespoke to your farm."

Apples are at least somewhat forgiving â the harvest window for those Granny Smiths is three weeks long, says Carter. For other fruit, such as berries, you might only have a few days.

"If a strawberry crop is on, you have to harvest it â otherwise your entire crop gets diseased very, very quickly," says Raymond Martin, co-founder and chief operating officer of FruitCast, a UK company that offers harvest forecasts to fruit growers here.

His firm offers growers crop predictions for strawberries and also raspberries, blackberries, blueberries and tomatoes. "We're moving on to grapes next year," adds Martin.

Don't experienced farmers know when their fruit will be ripe, I ask? Martin says they generally do â but not necessarily across their entire farm, which might be many acres in size, or have both outdoor and indoor growing areas. "We do exactly what the farmers could do but we just do it on a scale that they can't."

The system analyses footage of ripening fruit that can be captured by drones, someone walking up and down a field with a smartphone, or a camera mounted on a farm vehicle.

This year has been challenging, notes Martin, with hot weather and severe drought hitting much of the UK. This has stressed many fruit plants, putting them into thermal dormancy and slowing down fruit production.

There's only a small window for picking berries says Raymond Martin

The FruitCast model accounts for weather and irrigation conditions in its forecasts. The company says, "our forecasts land within 10% of actual picked volume one week out (90% accurate) and within 17% three weeks out (83% accurate). We guarantee less than 20% errorâ¦"

Angus Soft Fruits is one company that has worked with FruitCast. AI tech for forecasting fruit ripeness is not yet "a finished solution" says operations director Neill Finlayson: "This journey is still ongoing and, whilst significant progress has been made, the industry remains some way from achieving a fully integrated forecasting ecosystem."

Driscoll's, a California headquartered fruit seller with a big operation in the UK, also confirmed to BBC News that some of its independent fruit growers in the UK have used FruitCast's technology.

Yasaman Ghasempour (left) is developing ripeness detection techniques

Researchers are also exploring new ways of analysing fruit in intense detail. Many farmers already use handheld brix meters to gauge the sugar content of their crop. These devices work by measuring how much light has changed direction, or refracted, after passing through a liquid â indicating the volume of solids present.

Some such meters use infrared light, meaning there's no need to cut into the fruit to take measurements. But Yasaman Ghasempour at Princeton University says millimetre waves, high frequency radio waves, can penetrate deeper "They basically respond very well to humidity, water [and sugar]," she says.

She and her students have come up with a millimetre wave-based ripeness detector, external that could be used by farmers â or even customers searching for perfectly ripe fruit in shops. Her students tried it out at a local market in New Jersey. "[Staff] there got kind of scared that we were doing something shady," laughs Ghasempour.

Such fine-grained analysis of fruit, if currently unripe, could also help inform forecasts of when it will be ripe.

Jing Zhang says new tech has to prove its worth to farmers

But while some growers, such as Carter, are interested in trying out new tech, many will wonder whether investing in it is really worthwhile. "Adoption is very complicated," says Jing Zhang at North Carolina State University. "The grower has to have confidence in the research and whether or not it works."

Zhang has been working on a system to automatically count the number of blueberries, external captured in smartphone images of blueberry bushes.

The simplest systems need not be expensive. Kevin Wang at the University of Florida has developed another crop-counting tool that can harvest imagery, external from $100 (Â£74) drones.

In the future, such information could feed in to ripeness predictions. However, he points out that some farmers might be uneasy about sharing commercially sensitive information about their growing strategies, such as fertiliser and irrigation plans, with third parties and AI models.

Ben Palone is senior director of automation and commercialisation at Western Growers, an association that represents farmers in the western US. He says harvest forecasts have some potential as "an optimisation tool".

But farmers will likely continue to rely on human intelligence. "Growers like to have people in the mix to make some of those very critical decisions â especially when it comes to harvests," he says.

The critical tech staying safe by going underground

#217. Restaurant chains refocus on food quality to bring back diners

Juicier chicken tenders. Revamped sauces. Richer and creamier Frosties.

Across the restaurant industry, chains are turning their focus back to their food in the hopes that better quality will win back diners.

Over the last two years, consumers have dined out less frequently and grown pickier about where they choose to eat, based on data from Black Box Intelligence. In that time, "value" has been the buzzword du jour for restaurant executives, who have explained to analysts and investors that the word encompasses more than menu pricing alone.

Now, industry CEOs are turning to their corporate chefs to try to beat rivals on quality and taste, as they look to expand their definition of value. Discounts and one-off promotions can lift traffic for a time, but the actual product still matters for restaurants' long-term growth, as well as franchisee profitability.

From Wendy's to Chipotle Mexican Grill, chief executives touted new or upcoming improvements to menu items on their quarterly earnings conference calls in recent weeks.

"We think the product's improved and even better," Chipotle CEO Scott Boatwright told analysts on the company's call in late October, referring to the burrito chain's brisket, which returned to menus for a limited time Thursday.

For some chains, better food is a key step in ambitious turnaround plans.

For example, "quality food at real value" is one pillar of Wendy's five-part turnaround strategy that the chain unveiled on Thursday.

"We're also rebuilding our food offerings and have begun testing improvements to quality and craveability of our core items, including thicker, crispier bacon, a richer and creamier Frosty, whole leaf lettuce on sandwiches and improved salads," Wendy's CEO Bob Wright told analysts on the call Thursday.

That same day, the company reported its seventh straight quarter of same-store sales declines and publicly rejected a $1.4 billion takeover bid led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Partners. Wendy's said the proposal did not take into account the potential value that its turnaround plan could unlock.

There is precedent to suggest that better-tasting food can contribute to higher sales.

In 2009, Domino's Pizza changed its recipe, swapping out its sauce, adding garlic to its crust and switching to shredded mozzarella with a hint of provolone. Along with blunt commercials that compared its previous crust to cardboard and old sauce to ketchup, the changes helped to spur U.S. same-store sales growth of 9.9% the following year.

Now, some of the same team that led the Domino's turnaround is copying the playbook at Burger King's U.S. restaurants.

Part of the burger chain's multiyear comeback plan has focused on revamping its core menu items. It started earlier this year with its flagship offering, the Whopper, which received a better bun and a new mayonnaise. And in September, it rolled out an overhaul of its nuggets, switching to 100% white meat, crispier breading and a more flavorful marinade. It also reformulated its sauces.

"We've tried to look at our biggest categories first," Josh Kobza, CEO of Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International, told CNBC. "We've also thought about the ones where the fixes are more straightforward and less operationally involved."

Following the changes, Whopper sales were up by a double-digit percentage in the third quarter, Kobza said on the company's earnings call in late October. And nugget sales climbed more than 35%. Burger King said its U.S. same-store sales jumped 8.4% in the quarter.

Like Domino's, Burger King is highlighting diners' past gripes with its Whoppers and nuggets in its ads, which star Burger King U.S. president and former Domino's executive Tom Curtis.

Better-tasting food isn't just for chains that are in trouble.

Even Chili's, which has seen its same-store sales soar 52% over the past three years, is improving more of its existing menu options. After tackling its baby back ribs, burgers and nachos, it will turn next to salads and steaks, Brinker International CEO Kevin Hochman told CNBC.

"We're going to be doing more dry aging of our beef to make it more tender, versus doing it in a plant," he said. "It's a little bit more expensive, but we think the taste is going to be a whole lot better."

Chili's rival Applebee's launched a juicier chicken tender during its latest quarter, according to John Peyton, CEO of the chain's parent company Dine Brands. In 2027, Applebee's plans to overhaul more of its protein options.

"We think proteins are, number one, our biggest opportunity," Peyton told CNBC. "Number two, they're a big focus of the way people are thinking about eating right now, leaning into proteins and a little bit less with the carbs."

Chicken is also a focus for McDonald's, which is testing hand-breaded wings, strips and a sandwich in a handful of Chicago-area restaurants. It plans to expand the trial to more locations in the U.S. and Ireland in early 2027.

Moreover, McDonald's is trying to improve its coffee, with fresher beans and upgraded equipment, executives said at the company's investor day in September. Both chicken and coffee are fast-growing categories, offering more opportunities to lift sales than McDonald's signature beef burgers.

While the burger giant isn't in full turnaround mode like rivals Wendy's and Burger King, its U.S. business has struggled in recent quarters after its value strategy didn't gain the traction the company had hoped. In the third quarter, its U.S. same-store sales fell 0.2%.

In addition to menu tests, McDonald's is also leaning into training to improve the quality and consistency of its core menu items. Starting in October, the company launched "Make It Golden," a multiyear training program for corporate employees, restaurant workers and franchisees.

Ultimately, improved menu items mean little if the recipe changes aren't well executed.
